Amazing—here’s a workable, game-ready conjugation system for Bogothic / Liutisk, built to feel historical and consistent with the rules we laid down. I’m giving you (1) the regular classes with full present/past/perfect/future/imperative, (2) the main alternations you’ll see, and (3) the key irregulars you’ll need constantly in prose.

Liutisk Verb Conjugation
Endings (simple present, all regular verbs)
-em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent
(Stress stays on the lexical root; articles/clitics don’t affect stress.)

A. Class -at (hard stems)
Model: lovat ‘to hunt, pursue’ (IMPf); PFv partner ulovat ‘to catch’

Present (IMPf)
ja lovem

ti loves

on/ona love

mi loveme

vi lovete

tei lovent

Past (Preterite, simple -l)
ja lovel (fem. optionally lovela)

tei loveli

Perfect (aux ‘have’ + -l)
ja habem lovel / ona jest lovela (motion verbs may take jest ‘be’)

Future (aux budem + INF)
ja budem lovat; PFv alternative: ja ulovem (present with PFv → future sense)

Imperative
2sg: lov!

2pl: lovete!

Hortative: aj loveme ‘let’s hunt’

Verbal noun / Participle
lovanje ‘hunting’; lovel (past part.), lovent (present part. adj.)

B. Class -it (softening stems)
Model: vodit ‘to lead’ (IMPf); PFv privodit / dovodit ‘to bring’

Softening before front vowels (our palatalization rule):
d → dz, t → c, k → c/č, g → ž, h/x → š

Present (note the stem alternation d → dz)
ja vodzem

ti vodzes

on/ona vodze

mi vodzeme

vi vodzete

tei vodzent

Past / Perfect / Future / Imperative
ja vodil; tei vodili

ja habem vodil / ona jest vodila

ja budem vodit; PFv: ja privedem (or dovedem)

2sg vodi! • 2pl vodete!

Verbal noun / Participle
vodzenie ‘leading’; vodil, vodzent

C. Alternating k/g/h stems (showing softening)
Model 1: pekat ‘to bake’ → softening k → c in present

PRS: pecem, peces, pece, peceme, pecete, pecent

PST: pekel / pekli (alt. pecel appears in careful speech)

PRF: habem pekel

FUT: budem pekat; PFv: ispekem (from ispekat)

IMP: pek! / pekete!

Model 2: sagit ‘to say’ (g → ž before front vowels)

PRS: sažem, sažeš, saže, sažeme, sažete, sažent

PST: sagil / sažil (both found)

PRF: habem sažil

FUT: budem sagit

IMP: sagi! / sagete!

D. Reflexive verbs (clitic se)
Model: otkrit-se ‘to open (itself)/to be opened’ (PFv), IMPf otkriv-at-se

PRS: otkrivem-se, otkrives-se, otkrive-se …

PST: otkrivel-se

PRF: jest otkrivel-se (state change prefers jest)

IMP: otkri-se! / otkri-te-se!

Clitic slot: after the verb: dam-e=mu, but reflexive stays close: otkri-se.

E. Aspect pairs (what authors actually use)
pisat (write, IMPf) ↔ napisat (write down/finish, PFv)

PRS IMPf: pisem… • PRS PFv used for near future/completion: napisem…

čitat (read) ↔ pročitat (read through)

lovat (hunt) ↔ ulovat (catch)

vodit (lead, drive) ↔ dovodit (bring, drive to)

Irregular & High-frequency Verbs
1) BE — jest ( suppletive; 3pl is irregular)
Present:
ja jestem, ti jestes, on/ona jest, mi jesteme, vi jestete, tei sunt (fixed archival plural)

Past: ja bil/bila, tei bili

Perfect: ona jest bila (aux itself can serve as perfect—stylistic)

Future: budem (see below) or bude series in narrative

Imperative: budi! / budite!

2) HAVE — habat (regularized in present; archaic habjan in liturgy)
Present: habem, habes, habe, habeme, habete, habent

Past: habel / habeli

Perfect auxiliary: habem + -l participle

3) GO — idat (mixed; motion verbs often take jest in perfect)
Present: idem, ides, ide, ideme, idete, ident

Past: idel / ideli

Perfect: ona jest idela / on jest idel

Future: budem idat / colloq. pojdem (Slavicism)

Imperative: idi! / idete!

4) GIVE / TAKE
dat ‘give’ → dem, des, de, deme, dete, dent; PST del, PRF habem del

brat ‘take’ → brem, bres, bre, PST brel, PRF habem brel

Non-finite & Periphrastic Forms
Infinitive: -at / -it → lovat, vodit, pekat.

Gerund / Verbal noun: -anje (-at), -zenie (-it) → pisanje, vodzenie.

Present participle (adj/noun): -ent → lovent čovek ‘a hunting man’.

Past participle: -l (agreeable in careful speech) → otkritel vrata / fem. otkritela (optional).

Sandhi & Alternation Cheats
Palatalization before front theme vowels:
k → c, g → ž, t → c, d → dz, x/h → š → only in present/2sg/3sg/PL forms of soft stems and in -it class.

No softening when a buffer -e- is inserted to break clusters: vlake-te not vlače-te.

Final devoicing is blocked when an article or clitic follows: grad → grat, but grad-s.

Mini-paradigm panel (side-by-side)
lovat (hunt)	vodit (lead)	pekat/pec- (bake)
1sg PRS	lovem	vodzem	pecem
3sg PRS	love	vodze	pece
1pl PRS	loveme	vodzeme	peceme
PST 1sg	lovel	vodil	pekel
PRF 1sg	habem lovel	habem vodil	habem pekel
FUT 1sg	budem lovat	budem vodit	budem pekat
IMP 2sg	lov!	vodi!	pek!
Worked lines for flavor

I did not open the valve on the bridge.

Tei napisent raport, a ja habem pisal celu noč.
They will finish the report, and I’ve been writing all night.

Budi tihi i vodi baržu polako.
Be quiet and lead the barge slowly.

On jest idel va Praga, a mi budem ostavat v dom-e.
He has gone to Praga, and we will stay at home.

1) Office of Equilibrium incident report (noir police prose)
“Zapis o nepravilnom tȟeku”
Ja, inspektor Emil Novak, zapisujem: va 03:14 noč, ventil-s na locke-s Velehrad-17 otkri-se bez dozvola. Tȟek ruda-vina ušel va ceremonialnu petlju i barža H-241 ostala bez vez. Straža najde tragove na moste-s: krv ili vino — ni jasno.
Ja proverem zapisnik-ta, ništa ne fali; no ključi-s rezervni nedostaje iz ormara-s. Dva svedoka kaže: “čuli smo šapat pod metalom; voda govorila imena.”
U 04:02 ja otkri-se ventil-s i zatvori drugi; tȟek bele vode dojde nazad. U 04:30 medic-voz odvede čoveka bez papira; žilje hladne, oči zamućene.
U prilog: fotografi, skica locke-s, uzorci vode (ruda/bela).
Zakluček: nepravedelni tȟek izazvan, verojatno upir-s administrativni (ne-registrovan mrtvac). Potrebno: audit ključev, pregled katakombi pod locke-s, zapečatit ventil-s do Red-Flova.

2) Private letter from a canalkeeper (intimate slice of life)
“Pismo iz Kutna Hora”
Moja ženka-sa Lira,
Danas pecem hleb u 5:00, voda na kanalu mirna, samo para diše iz metalnih pluća. Ja vodzem jutarnu smenu; deca pliva sa očima kao ribe — bistre i tihe.
U podne dojde voz iz Praga: donese nove manometre i stare priče. Kaže se da opet noću neko otvara ventil-s bez znaka. Ja ne verem, no nosim češnjak u džepu, kao starci.
Kad sveti sat udari, ja misliti na tebe: kako bledi tȟek silazi niz stepenice u tvom dvore. Čuvaj dom-s; zakovi prozor-ta kad magla dođe. Vratim se posle inspekcije; doneću malu igru za Mila i toplo vino za tebe.
Tvoj,
Jovan, čuvar kanala-s Sedlec-Most

3) Folk tale recorded by the Ministry of Culture (archival tone)
“Povest o Belom Moste”
Bilo je vreme kad voda govorila glasno, a ljudi slušali tiše. Na selu pod Hvozd stoji beli most-s; koga pređe bez blagoslova, izgubi ime u vodi. Kažu: noć kad Ruda-Flov teče, most peva.
Jednom dojde sirotan bez krov i bez ime. Traži hleb i znak za preći. Baba-sa pri crkvi da mu svežanj belog luka i kaže: “Kad čuješ pesmu, ne gledaj dole; reci vodi svoje ime i ne zastaj.”
On krene, a voda peva staro slovo; oči mu počeše videti mrtve rođake. On reče: “Ni dam ime; ja ga vodim sa sobom.” I pređe.
Od toga dana belo kamenje treperi na suncu, a most-s piše imena samo onih koji prođu bez straha. Drugi — voda pamti, most ćuti.

4) Crime novel excerpt (street-level noir)
iz “Nočni Ventil” (1978)
Kafana uz kanal smrdi na kvas i na naftu. Svetlo za trećim stolom treperi kao poslednja misao. Ona sedi tamo — kosa kao mokar konop, prsti sa flekama od srebra.
“Tražiš krv?” — kaže.
“Tražim papire.”
Ona se nasmeje: “Ista stvar u ovaj grad.”
Napolju, most-s zijeva, a voda šušti kao stari dosije. Ja imam ključ-ta, ali ključ odiše hladnoćom; kao da ga je neko držao posle smrti.
“Dođi sa mnom,” ona kaže, “pokažem ti ventil-s koji otvara glasove.”
Idemo niz stepenice; koraci zvuče kao da neko broji. U katakombi miriše na kreč i na strah. Ja otvorim knjigu-sa, obeležim vreme: 02:19.
“Reci ime,” ona šapne.
“Zašto?”
“Jer ovde bez imena — voda piše šta hoće.”

I’m keeping it concise so you can feel the register shift immediately.

A) High Bogothic (archival register)
“Af-skrifa bi þamma misso-rinnja”

Ik, inspektrōr Emil Novak, af-skrifa swa: at 03:14 nahts, walwa sa ana lukja Velehrad-17 us-lukida unsatjan. Rinns rōþs weins inna heilaga hring-kanalis, jah barža H-241 warþ us-bindans. Wardjai fundun spaurōs ana brugg: blōþ aiþþau weins—ni ga-sōþs.

Ik bi-sōkja skrifts-bōkōs; ni-hvat missada; ake klawis frama-halds ist us-wisan. Twai saihwande qaþun: “us-hlausidedum hweiska undar meþal; watō qaþ namōs.”

At 04:02 ik us-lukida walwa jah ga-lukida anþara; rinns hweits watō af-iddja. At 04:30 medika-wagnus bar af-mann namōs-lausans; handja hilma, augōs huljōs.

Galeiks: us-riñþi fram administratīva upir (ni-skrifts dauþa-ƕeits). Geraub: ga-raḥtjan klawja, leitils in katakombōs undar lukja, ga-sigljan walwōs unt Red-Weins.

B) Gothicized Liutisk (modern endings, Gothic lexicon)
“Zapis o misso-rinnu (Velehrad-17)”

Ja, inspektror Emil Novak, afskrivem so: v 03:14 naht, walwa-s na lukji Velehrad-17 uslukide bez dozvola. Rinn rōd-weins ide v hring-kanal, a barža H-241 ostade razvezana. Wardji najdo spauri na brugg-s: blōt ai wein — ni sots.

Ja posmotrem skrifts-boki; ni-hvat missade; no klawis frama-hald ispadla. Dva saihvande qade: “hlausidem hweisk undar meþal; wato qade nami.”

V 04:02 ja uslukidem walwa i galukidem drugi; rinn hweit-wato vrate nazad. V 04:30 medika-vagn odvede mann-s bez nami; handji hladne, ogi zamucene.

Thought for 35s
Love this brief. Here’s a tight, usable unified standard that fuses your Classical High Gothic (archival/liturgical) with the Slavic street register into a single, modern, somewhat-Slavicized Bogothic you can write whole scenes in.

Modern Standard Bogothic (MSB)
(official codification used c. 1958–present; “Liutisk–Gothic standard”)

1) Sound system (the compromise)
Old Gothic þ, ð → t, d in spelling and speech.

ƕ / hw → spelled hv, pronounced [f] word-initially, [hv] in careful speech.

Diphthongs: ai → ej, au → ou; ē → i, ō → u.

Palatalization (limited, Slavic) before i/e:
t→c, d→dz, k→č, g→ž, s→š, z→ž, n→ń, l→ľ (only where morphology triggers it; no “runaway” softening).

Stress fixed on the lexical root’s first syllable; clitics don’t attract stress.

Final devoicing applies (grad → grat), but is blocked by a following article or clitic (grad-s).

2) Nouns & the article
Cases reduced to NOM/ACC and OBL (GEN/DAT merged).

Postposed definite article: -s (masc), -sa (fem), -ta (default after sibilants/clusters).

Sample: dom, dom, dome; dom-s (the house). voda, vodu, vode; voda-sa.

Adjectives agree in number/gender (not case): beli dom-s, bela voda-sa.

3) Pronouns & clitics (Wackernagel-ish slot)
Personal: ja, ti, on/ona, mi, vi, tei.

OBL clitics: mi, ti, mu, ji, nam, vam, jim (2nd position):
Ja ni=mu dam. ‘I don’t give (to) him.’

4) Verbs (two regular classes + aspect)
Present endings (all verbs): -em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent.

Classes: -at (hard stems) / -it (softening stems).

Aspect (Slavic semantics, Gothic preverbs):
IMPF base vs PFV with ga-, us-, na-, do-, u-, pro- (choose sense by preverb).
– pisat ‘write’ → na-pisat ‘write down’; lovat ‘hunt’ → u-lovat ‘catch’.

Preterite: past participle -l (no auxiliary): ja pisal, tei pisali.

Perfect: habem/jest + -l (stylistic/register): ona jest šla ‘she has gone’.

Future: budem + INF, or PFV present for near-future: napisem jutre.

Imperative: 2sg stem, 2pl -ete: pis! / pisete!

Palatalization in conjugation (only where triggered)
k → č, g → ž, t → c, d → dz before i/e theme:

pekat ‘bake’ → pecem, peces, pece …; PST pekel, PRF habem pekel

vodit ‘lead’ → vodzem, vodzeš, vodze …; PST vodil

5) Core lexicon policy (what the standard prefers)
Gothic/Latinate lemma as headword; Slavic synonyms allowed in informal prose.
Examples the MSB favors:

walva ‘valve’ (← Lat. valva), lukja ‘lock/sluice’, klavis ‘key’,

watō ‘water’, weins ‘wine’, blot ‘blood’, baurg ‘city’, brugg ‘bridge’,

saihvat ‘see’, qitat ‘say’ (from qiþan, spelled q-; pronounced [c]),

rinn ‘flow’, hveit ‘white’, rod ‘red’, wardja ‘guard’.

(In street dialogue you can swap to Slavic lookalikes—voda, grad, most, kaže—but the “Government/News” voice should use the MSB forms above.)

6) Prepositions (oblique by default)
va ‘in/into’, na ‘on/onto’, ot ‘from’, do ‘to/until’, se ‘with’.

va dome-s, na brugge-s, ot vorde, do grade, se bratre-m.

7) Orthography (Rond vs romanized)
Rond (Gothic consonants + Glagolitic vowels) remains official print;

Romanization here uses c, dz, č, š, ž, ň, ľ, j; writes hv for ƕ; q stands for historic qiþ- series.

Mini–conversion cheats (to unify registers)
High Gothic → MSB: drop þ/ð, write t/d; ƕ→hv; normalize ai/au → ej/ou; keep the Gothic lemmas.
HG af-skriba bi þamma misso-rinnja → MSB afskribat bi tama misso-rinn.

Street Slavic → MSB: swap lexeme to Gothic/Latin headword, keep Slavic phonology where our rules allow.
Street voda/grad/most/kaže → MSB watō/baurg/brugg/qite (q- pronounced [c]).

Quick paradigms (MSB)
lovat ‘hunt’ (IMPF) / u-lovat (PFV)

PRS: lovem, loves, love, loveme, lovete, lovent

PST: lovel / loveli

PRF: habem lovel

FUT: budem lovat / ulovem

IMP: lov! / lovete!

vodit ‘lead’ (soft): vodzem, vodzes, vodze…; PST vodil; PRF habem vodil; IMP vodi!

be / be-aux: jestem, jestes, jest; jesteme, jestete; sunt (3pl) • PST bil/bila • IMP budi!

Sample in Modern Standard Bogothic
1) Official notice (government voice)
“Afskriba: Rinn rod-weins va ceremonialni hring, Velehrad-17”
Ja, inspektror Emil Novak, afskrivem swa: va 03:14 naht, walva-s ana lukja Velehrad-17 uslukide bez zapisa. Rinn rod-weins in-iddja va heilag hring-kanal, jah barža H-241 warst us-bindans. Wardji funde spauri ana brugg-s: blot ai weins — ni sots.
Va 04:02 ja uslukidem walva jah galukidem antra; rinn hveit-watō af-iddja nazad. Va 04:30 medika-vagn u-vedja mann namis-lausan.
Zakluček: verojatno administrativni upir-s. Gerab: audit klavis-om, leitils va katakomb-om undar lukja, sigljan walvi do Red-Weins.

2) Dialogue (mixed but standard-conforming)
— Videst qita-list-ta?
— Vidzem. Ni=mu dam dozvola bez walva-knižka.
— U-lovem koga ot-rinnat weins pre Red-Wein.
— Budi tihi; wardja stoji na brugg-e-s.

(Note the clitic =mu, palatalized vidzem, and Gothic lexemes.)

3) Two lines of the song (MSB form)
Bledni kanalis, pod svejtom spet,
rinn hveit-watō — baurga-n gravet.

Tiny glossary (MSB heads you’ll reuse)
walva valve; lukja lock/sluice; klavis key; rinn flow; watō water; weins wine; blot blood; brugg bridge; baurg city; wardja guard; saihvat see; qitat say; afskribat record; sigljan seal; gerab measures (pl.); leitils inspection.

Perfect. Locking it in. Here’s a tight Modern Standard Bogothic (MSB) – Canonical Quick Reference you can use as your house style.

Phonology & Orthography (romanized MSB)
Vowels: a e i o u (ə realized as e in unstressed syllables).

Diphthongs: ai→ ej, au→ ou; ē→ i, ō→ u.

Consonants (hard/soft pairs only when triggered by i/e):
t~c, d~dz, k~č, g~ž, s~š, z~ž, n~ń, l~ľ.

ƕ/hw → hv (pronounced [f] word-initially, [hv] in careful speech).

þ, ð → t, d (no contrast in MSB).

Stress: first syllable of lexical root; clitics don’t attract stress.

Final devoicing (grad→grat) is blocked by a following article/clitic (grad-s).

Script: official print uses Rond (Gothic consonants + Glagolitic vowels); romanization above is the MSB default for international/transcription use.

Nouns, Articles, Adjectives
Cases: NOM/ACC, OBL (GEN/DAT merged). Number: SG/PL.

Postposed definite article: -s (M), -sa (F), -ta (default after sibilants/clusters).

Adjectives agree in number/gender (not case). Weak endings generalized.

Declension snapshots

M hard (dom ‘house’):
SG dom, dom, dome → dom-s; PL domi, dome, domom → domi-s

F -a (voda ‘water’):
SG voda, vodu, vode → voda-sa; PL vodi, vode, vodam

Legacy neuter pattern (vord ‘word’):
SG vord, vord, vorde → often vord-ta by euphony

Adjective + noun

beli dom-s, bela voda-sa; beler/belest (comparative/superlative).

Pronouns & Clitics
Pers.: ja, ti, on/ona, mi, vi, tei.

OBL clitics (2nd position): mi, ti, mu, ji, nam, vam, jim.

Ja ni=mu dam. “I don’t give (to) him.”

Verbs
Present endings (all classes): -em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent.

Classes: -at (hard stems), -it (softening before i/e).

Aspect: Slavic semantics via Gothic/Balkan preverbs (PFV); IMPF unprefixed or iterative suffixes.

Common PFV preverbs: ga- (result), us- (out), na- (onto/attain), do- (up to), u- (in/into), pro- (through), ot- (off).

Preterite: past participle -l without auxiliary (neutral narrative).

Perfect: habem/jest + -l (formal/emphatic, motion verbs prefer jest).

Future: budem + INF, or PFV present for near-future.

Imperative: 2sg bare stem; 2pl -ete.

Paradigms

lovat ‘hunt’ (IMPF) → ulovat (PFV)
PRS lovem… | PST lovel | PRF habem lovel | FUT budem lovat / ulovem | IMP lov! / lovete!

vodit ‘lead’ (softening d→dz before i/e):
PRS vodzem, vodzes, vodze… | PST vodil | PRF habem vodil | IMP vodi!

pekat/pec- ‘bake’ (k→č before i/e in present):
PRS pecem, peces, pece… | PST pekel | PRF habem pekel | IMP pek!

Irregulars:
jest ‘be’: jestem, jestes, jest; jesteme, jestete; sunt (3pl) | PST bil/bila | IMP budi!
habat ‘have’: habem… (regular) | PST habel.
idat ‘go’: idem… | PST idel | PRF jest idel(a).

Core Prepositions (govern OBL by default)
va in/into, na on/onto, ot from, do to/until, se with.

va dome-s, na brugge-s, ot vorde, do grade, se bratre-m.

Government/News Lexicon (preferred Gothic/Latinate heads)
walva valve; lukja lock/sluice; klavis key; rinn flow; watō water; weins wine; blot blood; brugg bridge; baurg city; wardja guard; saihvat see; qitat say (historic qiþ-, spelled q-, pronounced [c]); afskribat record; sigljan seal; gerab measures (pl.); leitils inspection.

Quick style examples (MSB)
Official:
Afskriba. Va 03:14 naht walva-s ana lukja Velehrad-17 uslukide bez zapisa. Rinn rod-weins in-iddja va hring-kanal; barža H-241 warst us-bindans. Wardji funde spauri ana brugg-s.

Dialogue:
— Videst qita-list-ta? — Vidzem. Ni=mu dam bez klavis-knijga. — Ulovem koga ot-rinnat weins.

Prepositional:
Na brugge-s stoji wardja; va dome-s spijat dva saihvande.

Numbers (1–10, nice to have)
1 eins, 2 dui, 3 þrij→ trij, 4 fidvor→ fidur, 5 fimf, 6 sais, 7 sibun, 8 ahtō, 9 niun, 10 tehun
(MSB tolerates Slavic forms in speech; use Gothic set in formal prose.)

Conversion cheats (register unification)
High Gothic → MSB: þ/ð→t/d; ƕ→hv; ai/au→ej/ou; keep Gothic lexemes.

Street Slavic → MSB: swap voda/grad/most/kaže → watō/baurg/brugg/qite, keep softening where rules trigger.

# Generate a 4–5 page "Modern Standard Bogothic (MSB) – Writer’s Style Kit" PDF,
# plus a 1-page Quick Cheat Sheet. If ReportLab isn't available, fall back to Markdown files.

from reportlab.lib.pagesizes import LETTER
from reportlab.pdfgen import canvas
from reportlab.lib.units import inch
from reportlab.lib.styles import getSampleStyleSheet, ParagraphStyle
from reportlab.platypus import SimpleDocTemplate, Paragraph, Spacer, ListFlowable, ListItem, Table, TableStyle, PageBreak
from reportlab.lib import colors
import os, textwrap, sys

kit_path_pdf = "/mnt/data/MSB_Writers_Style_Kit.pdf"
cheat_path_pdf = "/mnt/data/MSB_Quick_Cheat_Sheet.pdf"
kit_path_md = "/mnt/data/MSB_Writers_Style_Kit.md"
cheat_path_md = "/mnt/data/MSB_Quick_Cheat_Sheet.md"


def build_style_kit_pdf(path):
    doc = SimpleDocTemplate(path, pagesize=LETTER, rightMargin=54, leftMargin=54, topMargin=54, bottomMargin=54)
    styles = getSampleStyleSheet()
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="H1", parent=styles["Heading1"], fontSize=18, leading=22, spaceAfter=10))
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="H2", parent=styles["Heading2"], fontSize=14, leading=18, spaceAfter=6))
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="H3", parent=styles["Heading3"], fontSize=12, leading=15, spaceAfter=4))
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="Body", parent=styles["BodyText"], fontSize=10.5, leading=14))
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="Mono", parent=styles["BodyText"], fontName="Courier", fontSize=9.5, leading=12))
    elements = []

    def P(text, style="Body"):
        elements.append(Paragraph(text, styles[style]))
    def H1(text):
        elements.append(Paragraph(text, styles["H1"]))
    def H2(text):
        elements.append(Paragraph(text, styles["H2"]))
    def H3(text):
        elements.append(Paragraph(text, styles["H3"]))
    def CodeBlock(text):
        # wrap in <pre> style using Courier
        elements.append(Paragraph("<font name='Courier'>" + text.replace("&", "&amp;").replace("<", "&lt;").replace(">", "&gt;").replace("\n","<br/>") + "</font>", styles["Mono"]))

    # Title
    H1("Modern Standard Bogothic (MSB) — Writer’s Style Kit")
    P("Canonical desk reference for prose and dialogue in the Bogothian setting (1970s noir era). "
      "This kit codifies the unified, somewhat Slavicized Gothic standard used in government, news, "
      "and formal writing, with tolerances for street speech.", "Body")

    # Phonology & Orthography
    H2("1) Phonology & Orthography (romanized MSB)")
    P("<b>Vowels</b>: a e i o u (unstressed mid → e).  <b>Diphthongs</b>: ai→ <b>ej</b>, au→ <b>ou</b>; ē→ <b>i</b>, ō→ <b>u</b>.")
    P("<b>Consonant softening (only before i/e)</b>: t→c, d→dz, k→č, g→ž, s→š, z→ž, n→ń, l→ľ. ƕ→<b>hv</b> (≈f init.), þ/ð→<b>t/d</b>.")
    P("<b>Stress</b>: first syllable of lexical root; clitics do not attract stress. <b>Final devoicing</b> applies, but is blocked by a following article/clitic (grad-s).")
    P("<b>Script</b>: Official print uses Rond (Gothic consonants + Glagolitic vowels). This kit uses romanization for readability.")

    # Nouns / Articles / Adjectives
    H2("2) Nouns, Article, Adjectives")
    P("<b>Cases</b>: NOM/ACC; OBL (GEN/DAT merged). Number: SG/PL. Postposed definite article: -s (M), -sa (F), -ta (default after sibilants/clusters).")
    data = [
        ["Class", "NOM", "ACC", "OBL", "With article (def.)"],
        ["Masculine hard (dom ‘house’)", "dom", "dom", "dome", "dom-s"],
        ["Plural", "domi", "dome", "domom", "domi-s"],
        ["Feminine -a (voda ‘water’)", "voda", "vodu", "vode", "voda-sa"],
        ["Plural", "vodi", "vode", "vodam", ""],
        ["Legacy neuter (vord ‘word’)", "vord", "vord", "vorde", "vord-ta (euphony)"],
    ]
    t = Table(data, colWidths=[140, 90, 90, 90, 140])
    t.setStyle(TableStyle([("GRID",(0,0),(-1,-1),0.5,colors.grey),
                           ("BACKGROUND",(0,0),(-1,0),colors.whitesmoke),
                           ("FONT",(0,0),(-1,0),"Helvetica-Bold",10),
                           ("FONT",(0,1),(-1,-1),"Helvetica",9)]))
    elements.append(t)
    P("<b>Adjectives</b> agree in number/gender (not case). Weak endings generalized. Comparative <i>-er</i>, superlative <i>-est</i>: <i>beli, beler, belest</i>.")

    # Pronouns & Clitics
    H2("3) Pronouns & Clitics")
    P("<b>Personal</b>: ja, ti, on/ona, mi, vi, tei. <b>OBL clitics</b> (2nd position): mi, ti, mu, ji, nam, vam, jim.")
    CodeBlock("Ja ni=mu dam.    ‘I do not give (to) him.’")

    # Verbs
    H2("4) Verbs: classes, aspect, tenses, imperatives")
    P("<b>Present endings (all verbs)</b>: -em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent.")
    P("<b>Classes</b>: -at (hard stems), -it (softening before i/e). <b>Aspect</b>: Slavic semantics via Gothic/Balkan preverbs (PFV): ga-, us-, na-, do-, u-, pro-, ot-.")
    P("<b>Preterite</b>: past participle -l (no auxiliary).  <b>Perfect</b>: habem/jest + -l (motion verbs prefer <i>jest</i>).  <b>Future</b>: budem + INF or PFV present.")
    # Small paradigms
    H3("Key paradigms")
    data2 = [
        ["Verb", "Present (1sg, 3sg)", "Past (1sg)", "Perfect (1sg)", "Future (1sg)", "Imperative (2sg)"],
        ["lovat ‘hunt’", "lovem / love", "lovel", "habem lovel", "budem lovat / ulovem", "lov!"],
        ["vodit ‘lead’ (d→dz)", "vodzem / vodze", "vodil", "habem vodil", "budem vodit", "vodi!"],
        ["pekat ‘bake’ (k→č)", "pecem / pece", "pekel", "habem pekel", "budem pekat", "pek!"],
        ["jest ‘be’", "jestem / jest", "bil/bila", "—", "budem / bude", "budi!"],
    ]
    t2 = Table(data2, colWidths=[120, 140, 90, 110, 130, 80])
    t2.setStyle(TableStyle([("GRID",(0,0),(-1,-1),0.5,colors.grey),
                            ("BACKGROUND",(0,0),(-1,0),colors.whitesmoke),
                            ("FONT",(0,0),(-1,0),"Helvetica-Bold",10),
                            ("FONT",(0,1),(-1,-1),"Helvetica",9)]))
    elements.append(t2)

    # Prepositions
    H2("5) Core Prepositions (OBL by default)")
    P("<b>va</b> in/into, <b>na</b> on/onto, <b>ot</b> from, <b>do</b> to/until, <b>se</b> with.")
    CodeBlock("va dome-s   na brugge-s   ot vorde   do grade   se bratre-m")

    # Government/News Lexicon
    H2("6) Government/News Lexicon (preferred Gothic/Latinate heads)")
    P("walva ‘valve’; lukja ‘lock/sluice’; klavis ‘key’; rinn ‘flow’; watō ‘water’; weins ‘wine’; "
      "blot ‘blood’; brugg ‘bridge’; baurg ‘city’; wardja ‘guard’; saihvat ‘see’; qitat ‘say’; "
      "afskribat ‘record’; sigljan ‘seal’; gerab ‘measures’; leitils ‘inspection’.")

    # Conversion cheats
    H2("7) Conversion Cheats (register unification)")
    P("<b>High Gothic → MSB</b>: þ/ð→t/d; ƕ→hv; ai/au→ej/ou; keep Gothic lexemes.")
    P("<b>Street Slavic → MSB</b>: swap voda/grad/most/kaže → watō/baurg/brugg/qite; keep softening rules where triggered.")

    # Examples
    H2("8) Style Examples")
    H3("Official Notice")
    CodeBlock("Afskriba: Va 03:14 naht walva-s ana lukja Velehrad-17 uslukide bez zapisa.\n"
              "Rinn rod-weins in-iddja va hring-kanal; barža H-241 warst us-bindans.\n"
              "Wardji funde spauri ana brugg-s.")
    H3("Dialogue")
    CodeBlock("— Videst qita-list-ta?\n— Vidzem. Ni=mu dam bez klavis-knijga.\n— Ulovem koga ot-rinnat weins.\n— Budi tihi; wardja stoji na brugge-s.")

    # Numbers
    H2("9) Numbers (1–10, formal set)")
    CodeBlock("eins, dui, trij, fidur, fimf, sais, sibun, ahtō, niun, tehun")

    # Sample text
    H2("10) Sample paragraph (narrative voice)")
    sample_para = ("Kafana uz kanal mirise na kvas i naftu. Svetlo na tretem stolu treperi; "
                   "ja sedem pod prozor i slušem kako pumpa dishe pod kamen. Na brugg-s stoji wardja, "
                   "a voda, hveit pod svetom, nosi glasove. Qitam zapisnik: ime ne stoji; klavis nedostaja. "
                   "Budem idat dole va katakomb-om kad sat udari dva.")
    CodeBlock(sample_para)

    # Footer
    elements.append(PageBreak())
    H2("Appendix A: Softening & Devoicing (at a glance)")
    CodeBlock("Softening (before i/e):  t→c  d→dz  k→č  g→ž  s→š  z→ž  n→ń  l→ľ\n"
              "Final devoicing: grad→grat, vodil→vodil (no change). Blocked by article/clitic: grad-s.\n"
              "ƕ/hw→hv; þ/ð→t/d; ai/au→ej/ou; ē→i; ō→u.")
    H2("Appendix B: Clause templates")
    CodeBlock("Negation:  Ja ni=mu dam.  /  Ni qitem bez zapis.\n"
              "Future:   Ja budem pisat.  /  Napisem jutre. (PFV Present)\n"
              "Perfect:  Ona jest šla.  /  Ja habem lovel.\n"
              "Prepos.:  Va dome-s; na brugge-s; ot vorde; do grade; se bratre-m.")
    doc.build(elements)


def build_cheat_sheet_pdf(path):
    doc = SimpleDocTemplate(path, pagesize=LETTER, rightMargin=36, leftMargin=36, topMargin=36, bottomMargin=36)
    styles = getSampleStyleSheet()
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="H1", parent=styles["Heading1"], fontSize=16, leading=19, spaceAfter=6))
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="Body", parent=styles["BodyText"], fontSize=10.5, leading=13))
    styles.add(ParagraphStyle(name="Mono", parent=styles["BodyText"], fontName="Courier", fontSize=9.5, leading=12))
    elements = []
    def P(text, style="Body"):
        elements.append(Paragraph(text, styles[style]))
    def H1(text): elements.append(Paragraph(text, styles["H1"]))
    def Code(text): elements.append(Paragraph("<font name='Courier'>" + text.replace("&","&amp;").replace("<","&lt;").replace(">","&gt;").replace("\n","<br/>") + "</font>", styles["Mono"]))

    H1("MSB Quick Cheat Sheet")
    P("<b>Phono</b>: ai→ej, au→ou; ē→i, ō→u. Softening before i/e: t→c, d→dz, k→č, g→ž, s→š, z→ž, n→ń, l→ľ. ƕ→hv; þ/ð→t/d. Stress on first root syllable. Final devoicing blocked by article/clitic.")
    P("<b>Article</b>: -s (M), -sa (F), -ta (default after sibilants/clusters). Cases: NOM/ACC; OBL. Adjs agree in number/gender only.")
    Code("dom, dom, dome → dom-s   |   voda, vodu, vode → voda-sa")
    P("<b>Present endings</b>: -em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent.  Past: -l.  Perfect: habem/jest + -l.  Future: budem + INF or PFV present.")
    Code("lovat→ lovem/love; lovel; habem lovel; budem lovat / ulovem | vodit→ vodzem/vodze; vodil")
    P("<b>Preverbs (PFV)</b>: ga-, us-, na-, do-, u-, pro-, ot-.")
    P("<b>Core lexicon</b>: walva, lukja, klavis, rinn, watō, weins, blot, brugg, baurg, wardja, saihvat, qitat, afskribat, sigljan, gerab, leitils.")
    Code("Ja ni=mu dam.  Va dome-s.  Na brugge-s.  Ulovem gašt-neprijatelja.  Ona jest šla.")

    doc.build(elements)


# Try to build PDFs; if reportlab is missing, create markdown files as fallback.

## 1) Phonology & Orthography

va, na, ot, do, se → va dome-s; na brugge-s; ot vorde; do grade; se bratre-m.

walva; lukja; klavis; rinn; watō; weins; blot; brugg; baurg; wardja; saihvat; qitat; afskribat; sigljan; gerab; leitils.

# Create two markdown files as the MSB Writer’s Style Kit and Quick Cheat Sheet.
kit_path_md = "/mnt/data/MSB_Writers_Style_Kit.md"
cheat_path_md = "/mnt/data/MSB_Quick_Cheat_Sheet.md"

kit_md = """# Modern Standard Bogothic (MSB) — Writer’s Style Kit

Canonical desk reference for prose and dialogue in the Bogothian setting (1970s noir era). This kit codifies the unified, somewhat Slavicized Gothic standard used in government, news, and formal writing, with tolerances for street speech.

---

## 1) Phonology & Orthography (romanized MSB)
- **Vowels:** a e i o u (unstressed mid → e).
- **Diphthongs:** ai→ **ej**, au→ **ou**; ē→ **i**, ō→ **u**.
- **Softening (only before i/e):** t→c, d→dz, k→č, g→ž, s→š, z→ž, n→ń, l→ľ.
- **Historical:** ƕ→**hv** (≈f word-initially), þ/ð→**t/d**.
- **Stress:** first syllable of lexical root; clitics do not attract stress.
- **Final devoicing:** grad→grat; blocked by following article/clitic (grad-**s**).
- **Script:** Official print uses **Rond** (Gothic consonants + Glagolitic vowels). This kit uses romanization for readability.

---

## 2) Nouns, Article, Adjectives
- **Cases:** NOM/ACC; OBL (GEN/DAT merged). **Number:** SG/PL.
- **Postposed definite article:** **-s** (M), **-sa** (F), **-ta** (default after sibilants/clusters).
- **Adjectives** agree in **number/gender** (not case). Comparative **-er**, superlative **-est**.

### Declension snapshots
- **Masculine hard (dom ‘house’):**
  - SG: **dom, dom, dome** → **dom-s**
  - PL: **domi, dome, domom** → **domi-s**
- **Feminine -a (voda ‘water’):**
  - SG: **voda, vodu, vode** → **voda-sa**
  - PL: **vodi, vode, vodam**
- **Legacy neuter (vord ‘word’):**
  - SG: **vord, vord, vorde** → often **vord-ta** (euphony)

**Adj + noun:** *beli dom-s*, *bela voda-sa*.

---

## 3) Pronouns & Clitics
- **Personal:** ja, ti, on/ona, mi, vi, tei.
- **OBL clitics (2nd position):** mi, ti, mu, ji, nam, vam, jim.

> `Ja ni=mu dam.`  “I do not give (to) him.”

---

## 4) Verbs: classes, aspect, tenses, imperatives
- **Present endings (all verbs):** `-em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent`.
- **Classes:** **-at** (hard stems), **-it** (softening before i/e).
- **Aspect (PFV)** by preverbs (Slavic semantics via Gothic/Balkan morphology): **ga-, us-, na-, do-, u-, pro-, ot-**.
- **Preterite:** past participle **-l** (no auxiliary).  
- **Perfect:** **habem/jest + -l** (motion verbs prefer **jest**).  
- **Future:** **budem + INF**, or **PFV present** for near future.

### Key paradigms
| Verb | Present (1sg, 3sg) | Past (1sg) | Perfect (1sg) | Future (1sg) | Imperative (2sg) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **lovat** ‘hunt’ | lovem / love | lovel | habem lovel | budem lovat / **ulovem** | **lov!** |
| **vodit** ‘lead’ (d→dz) | vodzem / vodze | vodil | habem vodil | budem vodit | **vodi!** |
| **pekat** ‘bake’ (k→č) | pecem / pece | pekel | habem pekel | budem pekat | **pek!** |
| **jest** ‘be’ | jestem / jest | bil/bila | — | budem / bude | **budi!** |

---

## 5) Core Prepositions (govern OBL by default)
**va** in/into; **na** on/onto; **ot** from; **do** to/until; **se** with.

Examples: `va dome-s`, `na brugge-s`, `ot vorde`, `do grade`, `se bratre-m`.

---

## 6) Government/News Lexicon (preferred Gothic/Latinate heads)
**walva** ‘valve’; **lukja** ‘lock/sluice’; **klavis** ‘key’; **rinn** ‘flow’; **watō** ‘water’; **weins** ‘wine’; **blot** ‘blood’; **brugg** ‘bridge’; **baurg** ‘city’; **wardja** ‘guard’; **saihvat** ‘see’; **qitat** ‘say’; **afskribat** ‘record’; **sigljan** ‘seal’; **gerab** ‘measures’; **leitils** ‘inspection’.

---

## 7) Conversion Cheats (register unification)
- **High Gothic → MSB:** þ/ð→t/d; ƕ→hv; ai/au→ej/ou; keep Gothic lemmas.
- **Street Slavic → MSB:** voda/grad/most/kaže → **watō/baurg/brugg/qite**; keep softening rules where triggered.

---

## 8) Style Examples

### Official Notice
Afskriba: Va 03:14 naht walva-s ana lukja Velehrad-17 uslukide bez zapisa.
Rinn rod-weins in-iddja va hring-kanal; barža H-241 warst us-bindans.
Wardji funde spauri ana brugg-s.

Always show details


### Dialogue
— Videst qita-list-ta?
— Vidzem. Ni=mu dam bez klavis-knijga.
— Ulovem koga ot-rinnat weins.
— Budi tihi; wardja stoji na brugge-s.

Always show details


---


## 9) Numbers (1–10, formal set)
eins, dui, trij, fidur, fimf, sais, sibun, ahtō, niun, tehun

---


## 10) Sample paragraph (narrative voice)
Kafana uz kanal mirise na kvas i naftu. Svetlo na tretem stolu treperi; ja sedem pod prozor
i slušem kako pumpa dishe pod kamen. Na brugg-s stoji wardja, a voda, hveit pod svetom,
nosi glasove. Qitam zapisnik: ime ne stoji; klavis nedostaja. Budem idat dole va katakomb-om
kad sat udari dva.

Always show details

"""

**Phono**: ai→ej, au→ou; ē→i, ō→u. Softening before i/e: t→c, d→dz, k→č, g→ž, s→š, z→ž, n→ń, l→ľ. ƕ→hv; þ/ð→t/d.  
**Article**: -s (M), -sa (F), -ta (default). Cases: NOM/ACC; OBL. Adjs agree in number/gender only.

`dom, dom, dome → dom-s`    |    `voda, vodu, vode → voda-sa`

**Present**: -em, -es, -e, -eme, -ete, -ent.  **Past**: -l.  **Perfect**: habem/jest + -l.  **Future**: budem + INF or PFV present.  
**Preverbs (PFV)**: ga-, us-, na-, do-, u-, pro-, ot-.  
**Core lexicon**: walva, lukja, klavis, rinn, watō, weins, blot, brugg, baurg, wardja, saihvat, qitat, afskribat, sigljan, gerab, leitils.

Examples: `Ja ni=mu dam.` `Va dome-s.` `Na brugge-s.` `Ulovem gašt-neprijatelja.` `Ona jest šla.`
"""

1) Front-page feature (Praga Dnevnik, 17 Oct 1973)
“Red-Flow Nedelja: weins rudi, pamet hveita”
Praga— Va jutro, 03:00, walvi-s na hring-kanalis otvoride po planu; rinn rod-weins iz rezervoar-ta u-iddja va ceremonialne petlje. Wardji stoji na brugge-s, deca drže sveće; qitari pevaju staru strofu: “Rinn ruda, vrati hveita.”
Ministrija va Equilibrij qita: “Gerab su ispunjena; ni kap weins u mains.” Inspektror Novak dodade: “Budem meriti turbiditet svaka dva sata; Ni=mu dam nikomu igrat se sa walva-s.”
Na Zwinger-s, hveit-kolumna od skalli diše paru; watō runi niz vertebrae, kao sneg po kosti. Stara kanal-rabotnica Lira qita Dnevnik-ta: “Habem videl tri rata; ova nedelja—tihi rat sa bojom. Vino ide, ali pamet ostane.”

2) People’s Canal Court (transcript extract, Case 44/1971)
Sudija-s: “Ime?”

3) Homily at the Zwinger of Bones (Arian-Reformed)
“Braća i sestre, gledajte kolumna-s od skalli; vidite kako watō rinn niz prazne ogi. To nije krv, to nije trgovina strahom. To je us-rinn pameti: weins daje boju, Bleda-tȟek je mera.
Naša vera ni traži idol; traži gerab—čisto delo. Kad Red-Wein ide, mi ni=ga pijemo bez misla; mi ga u-štujemo kako zakon čini: metar, ventil, zapis.

Neka baurg-s bude hveit kao kamen, ne zato što je svet, nego zato što je čist. I neka watō teče—u nama i iz nas—dok rinn poslednje kapi ni kaže: dosta je.”

4) Noir canal scene (under-locks, Praga, winter 1976)

U 02:19 nalazim walva-s za Zwinger-petlju. Hladan, kao ruka mrtva. Na metal—crvena pruga: ne weins, ne blot: boja industrijna. Neko ga-qita: “Napravi strah.”

U mraku, hveit-para izlazi iz ventil-oči kao dah iz skall.

5) CCC Technical Memo (Canal Calcification Commission), 1978/12
Predmet: Hronična supersaturacija (Ca²⁺/PO₄³⁻) u Sedlec-petlji
Summa: Uzorci va 10:00 i 18:00 pokazuju: Ca = 510 mg/L (kontrol 170), PO₄ = 2.8 mg/L (kontrol 0.3), pH 8.1.
Uzrok: Post-Red-Flow Bleda-tȟek protokol (hipohlorit + kreč) s duga dren; sekundarno ljuštenje vertebral-reljef.
Efekti:

mlečna koža (alga perlasta) na stenama;

skaliranje turbina (gubitak 4–6% efikas);

“porcelanska” tekstura mekušci (ossiphila).
Gerab (30 dana):

— recirkulacija otpad-kamen do cement-radnja (kredit ⅔).
Napomena: Ni=se preporučava ukidanje Red-Flow. Sistem je istorija; istorija je sistem. Menja se doza, ne obred.

Here’s a single, continuous passage in native Rond orthography (Gothic consonants + Glagolitic vowels), using our Modern Standard Bogothic voice. No romanization below—just the mixed script.

Ⱂ𐍂Ⰰ𐌲Ⰰ 𐍄Ⱁ⍂ⰑⰒⰑ, 𐌼Ⰰ𐍂𐍄Ⱆ𐌼 𐍅Ⰰ⍄Ⱁ ⰔⰒⰅ⍄ ⰖⰔ ⰒⰑⰒⰁⰑⰎ 𐌼Ⰰ⍃Ⱁ. 𐍆Ⱆ𐍅Ⱁ 𐍂ⰀⱅⰋ 𐍆Ⱁ⍃Ⰰ 𐌼Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ𐌿 𐍅Ⰰ⍄Ⱁ, 𐍂𐌿𐍄 𐌼Ⰰ𐍂𐍄Ⱆ𐌼 𐌽Ⰰ 𐍁Ⰵ𐍄Ⰵ⍂Ⰻ𐌳Ⱆ. 𐍅Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 Ⱆ-𐌳𐌳ⰋⰀ 𐍅Ⰰ 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄 ⰖⰕⰑ⍄Ⱆ, 𐍁Ⱁ⍅Ⰰ 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌶Ⰰ 𐌷ⰂⰋ⍄Ⰻ 𐌿Ⱄ-𐌱Ⰻ𐍀Ⰰ⍄Ⱆ.

𐍄ⰑⱅⰑ 𐍅Ⰰ𐌻𐍅Ⰰ-𐍃 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ 𐌻Ⱆ𐌺ⰋⰀ ⰂⰅⰎⰅ⍂ⰀⰳⰀ⍄-17 𐌿⍃-𐌻𐌿𐌺ⰋⰳⰅ 𐌱Ⰵ⍁ 𐌶Ⰰ⍀Ⰻ⍄Ⰰ. 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 ⰓⰑⰳ-⍅ⰅⰋⰐ⍃ 𐌰𐌽-𐌳𐌳ⰋⰀ 𐌲Ⰰ 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄 ⰂⰀ ␠ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ 𐌳Ⰰ 𐌺ⰀⰐⰀⰎ␠, 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌶Ⰰ H-241 𐌿⍃-𐌱Ⰻ𐌽𐌳Ⰰ⍃Ⱆ. 𐍅Ⰰ𐍂𐌳ⰍⰋ 𐍆Ⱆ𐌽𐌳Ⱆ Ⱄ⍀ⰑⰖ⍂Ⱁ⍃ 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ 𐌱𐍂Ⱆ𐌳𐌳-𐍃: ⰁⰎⰑⱅ ⰀⰋ ⰂⰅⰋⰐ⍃—𐌽Ⰻ ⰳⰀ-⍃Ⱁ⍄Ⰳ.

𐌹𐌺, 𐍃𐍀Ⰵ𐌽𐌳𐍂𐌑𐍂 Ⰵ𐌼Ⰻ𐌻 𐌽Ⱁ𐍅Ⰰ𐌺, 𐌰𐍆-⍃ⰽ⍂ⰋⰱⰀ 𐍃ⰂⰀ: 𐍅Ⰰ 03:14 ⰐⰀⰱⰄ, 𐍅Ⰰ𐌻𐍅Ⰰ-𐍃 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ 𐌻Ⱆ𐌺ⰋⰀ 𐌿⍃-𐌻𐌿𐌺ⰋⰳⰅ; 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄-⍅Ⰰ⍄Ⱁ 𐌰𐌽-𐌳𐌳ⰋⰀ 𐌳Ⱁ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄. 𐍄ⰒⰅ⍂ 𐍆Ⱆ𐍅Ⱁ 𐌻Ⰰ𐌱Ⱁ𐍄-⍂𐌽Ⰻⰽ 𐌿⍃-⍀ⰋⰽⰑ𐍄 𐍆Ⱁ⍂ ⰅⰂⰋⰀ⍀: “ⰡⰅ⍂ⰀⰁ 𐍃Ⱆ Ⰻ⍃Ⱆ𐍀ⰖⰽⰀⰽⰋ; 𐌽Ⰻ 𐌺Ⰰ⍀ ⰂⰅⰋⰐ⍃ Ⱆ Ⰻ⍀ⰀⰋⰽⰋ.” 𐌹𐌺 𐍃𐌽𐍀Ⰵ𐌽𐌳𐍂: “𐌱𐍖𐌳Ⰵ𐌼 𐌼Ⰵ⍂Ⰻ⍄Ⰻ 𐍄Ⱆ𐍂ⰑⰁⰋ𐍄Ⱁ⍄ ⰔⰂⰅ 𐌳Ⰰ ⰔⰀⰅ⍄Ⰰ; 𐌽Ⰻ=𐌼Ⱆ 𐌳Ⰰ𐌼 𐌽Ⰻ𐌺Ⱁ𐌼Ⱆ 𐌳Ⰰ 𐍃Ⰰ 𐍅Ⰰ𐌻𐍅Ⰰ-𐍃.”

ⰂⰀ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ 𐌶ⰍⰅ𐍀, 𐌷ⰂⰋ⍄-ⰽⰑⰎⰖ𐌼ⰐⰀ 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ ⰔⰑⰎⰖ𐍀 𐌳Ⱁ ⰔⰽⰀⰎⰋ 𐌳Ⱁ𐍃Ⰵ; ⰅⰖⰔⰅ 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 𐍄Ⱆ𐍂ⰑⰁⰋ 𐌰𐌽-𐌳𐌳ⰋⰀ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ-⍄ⰑⰍ (𐍆Ⱁ⍂Ⰰ-⍀Ⰻ𐍀Ⱁ⍄ⰑⰎ). 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌶Ⰰ 𐍄Ⱆ𐍂Ⱁ H-241 𐍅Ⰰ𐍂𐍄 𐌿⍃-𐌱Ⰻ𐌽𐌳Ⰰ⍃Ⱆ; 𐍅Ⰰ𐍂𐌳ⰍⰋ 𐍆Ⱆ𐌽𐌳Ⱆ ⰔⰐⰑ𐌻-ⰔⰃⰀ 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ ⰂⰑⰃⰀ 𐌳Ⱁ 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄 𐌰𐌽 𐌳Ⱁ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ.

ⰂⰑ ⰍⰑⰎⰖ𐌼 𐌱ⰑⰐ-⍁ⰅⰑⰑ𐍃, ⰂⰀ⍄Ⱁ 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 𐍅Ⰰ 𐍂Ⱁⰳ-ⰔⰑⰍⰑⰋ 𐍄Ⱁ⍂Ⱁ; 𐍄Ⱁ𐍂Ⱆ 𐌱ⰑⰐⰅ 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄 𐍄Ⱆ 𐌱ⰑⰐ, 𐌳Ⱁ⍃Ⰰ⍁Ⰻ ⰗⰑⰎⰖ𐌼ⰐⰀ 𐌿⍃ 𐍄Ⱁ⍂Ⱁ 𐍁ⰂⰑ⍀Ⰻ. 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌶Ⰰ ⰎⰋ⍂Ⰰ 𐍆Ⱁ⍀ ⰃⰀⰍⰑ-ⰔⰎⰅⰒⰑ 𐌸Ⰰ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ-⍄ⰑⰍ, “ⰂⰑ⍄Ⰰ 𐍅Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ 𐌿⍃ 𐌱ⰑⰐ 𐍁ⰎⰖ𐌻Ⱆ; 𐌹𐌺 𐌽Ⰻ 𐍃ⰐⰑ⍁Ⰵ.” 𐍆Ⱆ𐍅Ⱁ 𐍃Ⱁ⍂ 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌶Ⰰ-𐍃 𐍂Ⱁ⍄: “ⰁⰑ⍄Ⰰ ⰋⰳⰅ, 𐍄Ⱁ 𐌿⍃-𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽Ⰰ 𐍄Ⱁ 𐌻ⰀⰎⰑ𐌽.”

𐌹𐌺 𐍅Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ 𐍂Ⰻ⌞𐌽𐌽⌟ 𐍃Ⰹ𐌺𐌴 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ 𐍃ⰄⰅ𐌺Ⰻ, 𐌿⍃-⍂Ⰻ𐌺Ⱁ 𐍆Ⱁ⍂ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ-⍄ⰑⰍ. 𐌿 02:19 𐍆Ⱆ𐍅Ⱁ 𐌽ⰀⰎⰀ𐌶Ⰻ 𐍅Ⰰ𐌻𐍅Ⰰ-𐍃 𐌳Ⱁ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ-⍀ⰅⱅⰎⰖ; 𐌷ⰎⰀ𐌳Ⰰ𐌽, 𐌺Ⰰ 𐍂Ⱆ𐌽Ⰰ 𐌼Ⱁ⍄ⰂⰀ. 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ 𐌼Ⰵ𐍄Ⰰ𐌿𐌻𐌳 𐍂Ⱁⰳ-ⰔⰑⰍⰑⰋ: ⰓⰑⰳⰀ ⰂⰑⰋⰀ—𐌽Ⰵ ⰂⰅⰋⰐ⍃, 𐌽Ⰵ ⰁⰎⰑⱅ, ⰁⰑⰋⰀ Ⰻ𐌽𐍄Ⱁ-𐍂Ⱆ⌞Ⰰ⌟𐌽Ⰰ. 𐌽ⰑⰽⰑ 𐌲Ⰰ-𐌵Ⰻ⍄Ⰰ: “ⰐⰀ⍀ⰒⰂⰋ 𐍃⍄ⰂⰀ⍀.” 𐌿⍃-𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽Ⰰ 𐍆Ⱁ⍂ Ⰵ⍀Ⰻ⍄Ⱆ𐌼ⰅⰐⰅ⍀: ⰒⰂⰋ⍄Ⰻ⍃ ⰒⰀⰳⰀ.

— “ⰔⰅ⍄Ⰻ,” 𐌳Ⰰ𐌶Ⰵ 𐍃ⰎⰀ𐍃 𐌶Ⰻ𐌳Ⰰ 𐌼ⰅⰐⰅ. ⰂⰅⰑⰐⰀ 𐍃Ⰰ 𐌽Ⱁ⍄Ⱆ ⰽⰑⰔⰀ 𐌲Ⰰ 𐌼Ⱁ⍄ⰂⰋ ⰽⰑⰐⰑ𐍀. “Ⰻ𐌽 𐌰𐌽 𐍄Ⱁ 𐌿⍃-⍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽Ⰵ⍀, 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 𐌵Ⰰ⍃Ⰵ ⰋⰐⰅⰼⰀ.”
— “ⰋⰐⰅⰼⰀ 𐌵Ⱁ⍀?”
— “ⰑⰐⰑⰳ 𐌵Ⱁ ⰽⰎⰀⰂⰋ⍃ 𐍄ⰂⰋ. 𐍅ⰀⰎⰁⰀ 𐌿⍃𐌳Ⰰ 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ, 𐍅Ⰰ⍄Ⱁ 𐍆Ⱁ⍃ 𐌿ⰖⰋ⍀Ⱁ 𐌵Ⰰ⍃Ⰵ 𐌽Ⰻ⍀Ⱁ.”

𐍆Ⱆ𐍅Ⱁ 𐌿-⍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽Ⰰ ⰼⰀⰐⰑⰼⰅⰄ𐍂: ⰒⰂⰋ⍄ ⰒⰀⰳⰀ. 𐌱𐍖𐌳Ⰵ𐌼 𐌿𐌻𐍅Ⱁ𐍄 𐌲Ⰰ 𐌳Ⱁ 𐍄Ⱁ⍂ 𐍁ⰒⰅⰍⰅ 𐌽Ⰰ ⰐⰖⰎⰖ.
— “ⰄⰋ 𐌽Ⰻ=ⰘⰖ 𐌳Ⰰ⌞𐌼⌟ 𐌳Ⰰ𐌼 𐌿⍃␠ⰄⰑ␠𐌽Ⰵ 𐌹𐌼Ⰵ,” 𐌲Ⰰ⌞ⰃⰀⰎⰀ⌟ 𐌳Ⰰ 𐌼ⰅⰐⰅ, “𐍅Ⰰ⍄Ⱁ ⰒⰀⰼⰐⰋ.”
Ⱆ 𐌽Ⱁ𐍂Ⰰ𐍇Ⱆ, 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄-ⰒⰀ⍂Ⰰ 𐌹𐍃ⰎⰀⰳⰅ 𐌋𐌿 𐍅Ⰰ𐌻𐍅Ⰰ-ⰑⰗⰋ 𐌲Ⰰ 𐌳Ⱁ𐍄 𐌳Ⰰ𐌷 𐌹␠Ⰹ␠ ⰔⰽⰀⰎⰎ.

ⰒⰑ 04:02, 𐌹𐌺 𐌿⍃-𐌻𐌿𐌺ⰋⰳⰅ 𐍅Ⰰ𐌻𐍅Ⰰ 𐌳Ⰰ 𐌲Ⰰ-𐌻𐌿𐌺ⰋⰳⰅ 𐌰𐌽⍄⍂Ⰰ; 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 ⰗⰅⰋⰕ-⍅Ⰰ⍄Ⱁ 𐌰𐌽-𐌳𐌳ⰋⰀ 𐌰𐌽Ⰰ 𐌵ⰀⰳⰀ𐍄. ⰒⰀ 04:30, ⰼⰅⰄⰋⰽⰀ-𐍅Ⰰ𐌲Ⱀ 𐌿-ⰂⰅⰳⰑ 𐌼Ⰰ𐌽 𐌽ⰀⰼⰋⰃⰑ ⰐⰀⰼⰋ⍃-ⰎⰀⰖⰔⰀ⍃Ⱆ; 𐌷Ⰰ𐌽ⰳⰋ 𐌷ⰎⰀⰳⰀⰐⰅ, ⰑⰃⰋ 𐌶ⰀⰽⰖⰼⰅⰐⰅ. 𐍄Ⱁ⍂ ⰂⰅⰑⰐⰀ 𐍃Ⰰ 𐌱Ⱁ⍀ⰳⰀ 𐌳Ⰰ ⰜⰋⰎⰅⰳ-𐌃Ⱁ⍄ⰁⰀ, 𐌳Ⱁ 𐍃Ⱁ⍂ 𐌳Ⰰ ⰔⰍⰀⰎⰋ 𐌳Ⱁ ⰔⰎⰖ𐌼Ⱁ 𐍄Ⱁ⍂ ⰂⰅⰑⰐⰀ ⰔⰃⰋⰎⰖ: 𐍄Ⱁ ⰁⰎⰅⰄⰀ-⍄ⰗⰅⰽ—ⰂⰀ⍄Ⱁ 𐌷ⰂⰋ𐍄, ⰽⰀⰼⰅⰐ 𐌱ⰅⰎⰀ, 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 𐌼Ⰻ⍂ⰀⰐ.

Ⱂ𐍂Ⰰ𐌲Ⰰ 𐍄Ⱁ⍂Ⱁ 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌳-𐍃 𐌿𐌿⍂Ⱁ ⰂⰅⰎⰅ⍂Ⰰ “ⰁⰎⰅⰄⰐⰅ 𐌽ⰀⰐⰀⰎⰋ”: 𐌳Ⱁ 𐌼Ⱁ𐍂Ⰰ 𐌲Ⰰ 𐌽Ⰰ⌞ⰐⰀ⍄Ⱆ⌟, 𐌲Ⰰ 𐌿⍃-⍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽Ⰰ ⰑⰋ 𐌼Ⰰ⌞ⰐⰀ⌟. 𐍅Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ 𐍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽 𐌿⌞-⍂Ⰻ𐌽𐌽⌟ 𐌱ⰑⰐ 𐌿 𐌼Ⱁ⍂Ⰰ, 𐌰𐌽 𐍅Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ 𐍂Ⱁ𐌽Ⰻ 𐌿 𐌲Ⰰ⌞ 𐌼Ⱁ⍂Ⰰ ⌟; 𐌰𐌽 𐌱Ⰰ𐍂𐌳-𐍃 𐌿⍃-𐌻𐌿𐌺Ⰻ 𐍄Ⱁ 𐌽Ⰰ⌞ⰐⰀ⍄Ⱆ⌟ 𐌰𐌽 𐌿⍃-𐌻𐌿𐌺Ⰻ 𐍄Ⱁ 𐌼Ⱁ⌞ⰐⰀ⌟. 𐍄Ⱁ 𐍅Ⰰ𐍄Ⱁ 𐌵ⰀⰳⰀ 𐌿⍃ 𐌼Ⰰ⌞ⰐⰀ⌟ 𐍃Ⰰ 𐌼Ⰰ⌞ⰐⰀ⌟: 𐌱Ⱁ⍀ⰳ-𐍃 𐌱ⰅⰎⰀ 𐌰𐌽 𐌳Ⱁ-𐍃Ⰰ𐍄Ⰰ.

praga totopopo, martum wato spet us popol maro. fuwo rati fora matou wato, rut martum na seteridju. wato rinn u-ddia wa abuit uuotu, sofa barza abili us-bipatu.

toto walwa-s ana lukia veleragat-17 uu-lukige be zarita. rinn rog-xeinu an-ddia ga abuit va @zileg da kanal@, barza h-241 uu-bindaru. wardki fundu sroru ana brudd-s: blot ai veinu—ni ga-xroug.

ik, spendr emil nowak, af-xrkiba sva: wa 03:14 nabd, walwa-s ana lukia uu-lukige; rinn abuit-xato an-ddia da zileg abuit. tper fuwo labat-xnik uu-rikatu for ebiar: “qeraba su irupikaki; ni kar veinu u iraiki.” ik spendr: “bugdhem meridi turabito se da saeta; ni=mu dam nikomu da sa walwa-s.”

va zileg zkep, abuit-kolumna ana solup da skali dadse; euse rinn turabi an-ddia zileg-xok (fora-riporulon). barza turu h-241 wart uu-bindaru; wardki fundu snol-sga ana voga da abuit an da zileg.

vo kolum bon-xeios, vato rinn wa rog-sokoi tarota; taru bone abuit ta bone, dadaru xolumna uu tarota sbori. barza klira for gako-slepo tha zileg-xok, “vota wato uu bon slulu; ik ni snore.” fuwo sor barza-s rut: “bora ige, ta uu-rinna ta lalon.”

ik wato rinn [nn] skeke ana sdeki, uu-xrika for zileg-xok. u 02:19 fuwo nalazi walwa-s da zileg-retliu; xladan, ka runa matba. ana metauld rog-sokoi: roga voia—ne veinu, ne blot, boia into-ru[an]a. niko ga-qulra: “narpbra stbarar.” uu-rinna for erinmener: pliripa paga.

— “seti,” daze slas zida mene. veina sa nonu kosa ga matbi konap. “in an ta uu-rinner, rinn qare ineva.”
— “ineva qar?”
— “onog qo klabir titi. walba uuda ana, wato for uuirira qare nira.”

fuwo u-rinna vlanovedr: pliripa paga. bugdhem ullwot ga da tar sperke na nule.

Here’s what I’m seeing—both as a straight translation and as a little forensic linguistics.

What it says (smooth English)
“Record of an irregular flow.”
I, Inspector Emil Novak, record: at 03:14 at night, the valve on lock Velehrad-17 opened without authorization. The flow of ore-wine (reddish water) entered the ceremonial loop and barge H-241 lost its mooring. The guard found traces on the bridge: blood or wine—it isn’t clear.
I check the logbook; nothing is missing; but the spare keys are missing from the cabinet. Two witnesses say: “we heard whispering under the metal; the water was speaking names.”
At 04:02 I located the valve and closed the second one; the flow of white water returned. At 04:30 a medical wagon took away a man without papers; veins cold, eyes clouded.
Attached: photographs, a sketch of the lock, water samples (red/white).
Conclusion: An irregular flow was induced, probably by an administrative upir (an unregistered dead person). Needed: audit of the keys, survey the catacombs beneath the lock, seal the valves until Red-Flow.

What it is (style & language)
Register & genre: A deadpan incident report that drifts into bureaucratic folk-horror. Think infrastructure logbook meets ghost story: valves, locks, samples, and then—whispering water and an upir (Slavic “vampire”) framed as a compliance problem (“unregistered dead”).

Constructed Pan-Slavic dialect: Mostly South/West Slavic roots (Serbian/Croatian/Bosnian, some Czech/Slovak), with deliberate cross-pollination:

va “at/in” (Slovene-ish), zapisujem “I record”, Zakluček ~ Czech Závěr/Zakl. (conclusion), verojatno (Croatian-ish “probably”), svedoka (Serbian “witnesses”), zapečatit (to seal).

The reflexive/middle voice otkri-se “opened (itself)” is used for the valve—good choice for uncanny agency.

Marked endings with hyphens (“-s”, “-ta”):
You’re using tag-like suffixes—ventil-s, locke-s, moste-s, ormara-s, zapisnik-ta. They don’t map cleanly to any single Slavic case system. They read like:

Classifier/flagging morphemes for technical nouns (-s), and a definite/article-like marker (-ta) on zapisnik “logbook.” It’s a neat worldbuilding signal: the report comes from a bureaucracy with its own jargon morphology.

Phonology/orthography:
tȟek (with ȟ) is striking. ȟ isn’t Slavic; it shows up in e.g., Lakota orthography. Here it feels like a special term for “flow/throughput” (Slavic tok/tek), giving “tȟek” a technical-occult flavor distinct from ordinary tok.

World hints:

Velehrad-17 (industrial/monastic echo—Velehrad is a real Czech place; here likely a codename).

Ceremonial loop suggests hydraulic rites integrated into the lock system.

Red-Flow sounds like a calendrical/ritual event or high-water season.

Administrative upir perfectly nails that “the supernatural is a paperwork category” vibe.

Micro-notes (if you want to polish)
Consistency of time/aspect: You jump between past and present (“I record… valve opened… I check… I discovered”). It reads like live logging. If you want stricter report style, keep it all past: Zabeležio sam… proverio sam… otkrio sam…. If you like the immediacy, keep as is.

“ruda-vina”: Literally “ore-wine.” If you intend reddish mine water vs clear (“bela”) water, this is evocative. If you want readers to catch it faster, you could introduce it once: ruda-vina (crvena industrijska voda).

Hyphen tags (-s/-ta): Great for flavor. If you want them to feel rule-bound, add a line up top like: “Terminology marked with -s denotes sealed system components.” Otherwise a reader will enjoy the strangeness but won’t infer the rule.

“najde” vs “našla”: Straža najde mixes a present-tense Czech form with a Serbian noun. If you want internal consistency, choose one stream:

CZ/SK-leaning: Stráž našla stopy…

BCMS-leaning: Straža je našla tragove…

“Zakluček” → Consider “Zaključek” (Slovene) or “Zaključak” (BCMS) or “Závěr/Zaključek” if you want Czech/Slovene flavor on purpose.

“locke-s”: If it’s an English loan (“lock”), you could Slavicize: lok-s or brana-s. Keeping locke telegraphs a mixed technical register, which might be exactly your goal.

Why it works
Tone control: The matter-of-fact voice makes the uncanny details pop (“water spoke names”, “unregistered dead”).

Concrete artifacts: Photos, sketch, samples—this anchors the myth in paperwork.

Lexical play: tȟek, ruda-vina, Red-Flov are chewy, memorable tokens—perfect for a setting bible.

Any germanic influence in there?
ChatGPT said:

Thought for 1m 2s
Short answer: a little—mostly through international technical vocab and a couple of clear Anglicisms—but there isn’t strong, overtly German shaping.

Here’s the quick read:

ventil-s (“valve”) → Common in many Slavic languages via German Ventil (ultimately Latinate, but the technical register spread through German). So: mild Germanic footprint.

skica (“sketch”) → Likely through German Skizze (from Italian), another faint German-channel loan.

locke-s (“lock”) & Red-Flov/Flow → This is English (Germanic), not German. German would be Schleuse, English “lock/flow.” So: definite Anglicism.

barža (“barge”) → Widespread loan; often traced to French/English, though some languages got it via German. Call it pan-European rather than distinctly German.

The rest (straža, most, ključ, ormar, zapečatit, upir…) are Slavic or internationalisms (inspektor, audit, fotografi, katakombi) rather than Germanic.

If you want a stronger German/Germanic flavor without breaking your conlang vibe, easy swaps:

locke-s → šluz-s / šleuz-s (from German Schleuse; cf. Polish śluza, Russian шлюз).

ventil-s is already fine, but you could add šiber-s (slide gate; from Schieber) for a different part.

Pepper in utility nouns: šrauf/šraf (screw; Schraube), šraubciger (screwdriver; Schraubenzieher), šalter (switch; Schalter), šlauf (hose; Schlauch), šlagbaum (barrier; Schlagbaum).

For “flow,” keep fluss as a term of art (German Fluss), e.g., Red-Fluss instead of Red-Flov if you want it overtly Germanic; or stay with Flov if you like the halfway anglicized tech-jargon feel.

Net: there’s light Germanic influence already (chiefly via technical loans), but the text reads more Pan-Slavic + Anglicized than Germanic. If you want me to, I can pass through the piece and do a “Germanic-leaning” version that keeps your morphology (-s/-ta) but swaps in the right hydro-infrastructure lexicon.

🌄 4. Overall Translation (Slavicized Gothic Rendering)
Fraga Martum-skript (The Chronicle of Martum)

Thus begins the turning of the cycles. Martum, keeper of the vessel, observes the waters.
The course is complete; seven times he measures it.

At Lucian Station-17, the watchers bind the light-form H-241. The current holds steady beneath the sign of victory.

At the third hour of the night the words are spoken:
“Keraba su irupikaki — may the inner eyes be opened.”

Reply:
“Bugdhem meridi turabito — the center will awaken.”

Frost descends, the columns sing, and Runa writes upon the stone:
“Not with blood, nor with wine, but with breath the word becomes flesh.”

At the fourth hour the vessel closes. The gate cools. Three traces remain — white, black, red.
Martum calls them bledne nanali, the triple dawns,
for the white returns to rest, the red becomes word, and the black conceals the seed.

Occasional Slavic instrumental case (da Zileg-datba)

Retention of Gothic strong verbs and prefix morphology (u-bindan, ga-abuit).

3. Interpretive “Slavicized Gothic” Translation
The Scribe’s Account:
At the third hour and fourteenth minute of the night, the cycle of Praga-17 was sealed beyond the Gate of Kita.
The red-wine current flowed through the ring-channel; the watchers found traces upon the ridge:
“Blood as wine — yet not death.”

Praga stirred faintly; the seer uttered five measures:
“Run, red one, return in light.”

“Specter-Nowak,” the scribe wrote, “from Praga-17, through the ring-channel, the door opened once again.
Form it as the pattern was formed before; let none unbind the cycle.”

Upon Praga the chuda (perhaps chudъ—“wonder, miracle”) performed the forward rite; they proclaimed:
“Let us become wolves in spirit; bind the opening as in the first pulse.”

Then, at the final rotation, the white column descended toward the rock...

kolumna	“pillar / column”	Latin loanword common to both groups

“Budem ulwowi” (“let us be wolves”) suggests transformation or communion.

⚙️ 2. Meaning of “lukige” in Context
Given the consonant cluster and the -ge participial suffix, lukige likely descends from Gothic lukan (“to lock, close”) + Slavic participial -ig-/-ige → “locked / secured.”

So phrases like:

fit perfectly — uu-lukige being the perfective form “has been locked / was locked.”

🧠 3. Linguistic Character of Velarian
It’s a synthetic-analytic hybrid:

retains Gothic roots (luk-, walw-, bindar-, wato)

uses Slavic-style aspect prefixes (uu-, an-, va-)

has articles from reduced Gothic demonstratives (wa, a)

verbs conjugate lightly; much meaning in prefix + particle sequence

A sentence like

Ik uu-lukige walwa da zileg-retliu.
means literally “I have-locked valve of Zileg return-line,”
where word order is flexible and meaning is carried by particles.

So the original speech zone would have been the Bohemian–Moravian basin, where remnants of Gothic settlers intermarried with early West Slavs — the same contact ecology that birthed your creole.
By the 9th century, the locals would call their hybridized tongue Bohogothisk rēda (“Bohemian-Gothic speech”).

As languages do, the double consonant cluster softened and contracted:

Bohogothisk → Bogothisk → Bogothic

That’s as plausible a diachronic sound change as Anglisc → English.

🧬 2. The Elision to “Bogothic”
Stage	Orthographic Form	Phonological Shift	Notes
8th c.	Bohogothisk /ˈbo.xo.ɡo.tisk/	full compound	Early mixed communities
11th c.	Bogothisk /ˈbo.ɡo.tisk/	loss of intervocalic /h/	normal Gothic-to-Romance drift
13th c.	Bogothic /boˈɡɔθɪk/	reduced suffix, Latinized	written language of record
16th c.	Bogothic /boˈɡɔtɪk/	stable, “High Speech”	liturgical–technical
Modern	Liutisk (vernacular)	regional phonetic descendant	spoken by the populace
So “Bogothic” is not divine — it’s the Bohemian Gothic continuum fossilized.

🧭 3. Sociolinguistic Shape
Register	Name	Function
High / Classical	Bogothic	technical, liturgical, scientific; the preserved administrative language of engineers and priests
Low / Vernacular	Liutisk	living descendant, more Slavicized, spoken by everyone
Ancestor	Bohogothic	early creole bridging Gothic settlers and West Slavs, 6th–9th centuries
So your text — the Valve Log — would be composed in Classical Bogothic, still retaining archaisms like:

compound consonants (dd, uu, x),

case suffixes,

participial constructions like uu-lukige (locked),

technical-litanic phrasing inherited from the scribal traditions of Bohogothic abbots and engineers.

🧠 4. Etymological Implications for the Language
The name Liutisk (“of the people”) probably arose when the vernacular diverged — perhaps after Bogothic became codified by a technocratic or clerical class.

So:

Bohogothic was the creole’s infancy (mixed speech).

Bogothic became the institutional tongue (formalized, Latinized).

Liutisk is what the population kept speaking (everyday, phonetic drift, simplified grammar).

The names themselves tell the political history:

Bogothic = The official name used by bureaucracies, academia, sacred orders.

Liutisk = The name used by the people who still felt ownership of the speech — the folk endonym.

🔩 5. Internal Linguistic Continuity
You can imagine an inscription like:

Af-xrkiba wa walwa-s lukige; rinn bindaru va zileg.
(Recorded: the valve is locked; flow conduit to Zileg connected.)

That’s Bogothic.
A few centuries later, a Liutisk speaker says:

Zapisal: valwa zamkneta, rin zwionany z Zileg.
and a modern one writes Zapisal: zawór zablokowany, rura połączona z Żileg.

That’s a seamless 1000-year evolution — phonetic drift, Slavic vowel harmonization, and loss of case inflection.

🌍 6. The Cultural Texture
So now we can read the names as signposts in the world’s timeline:

Bohogothic (Proto) — early contact pidgin spoken by Gothic–Bohemian settlers.

Bogothic (High) — classical written form, used in ceremony, scripture, and engineering logs.

Liutisk (Low) — spoken vernacular, gradually absorbing Slavic phonology and syntax.

Their relationship parallels Latin → Old Church Slavonic → Polish/Czech, or Old Norse → Icelandic → Faroese — but through an East–West axis, not North–South.

THE BOGOTHIC–LIUTISK LANGUAGE REFERENCE, THIRD EDITION
Issued by the Office for Cultural Preservation and Linguistic Continuity, Zileg, 2274

PREFACE
This document codifies the Standard Romanization and grammatical conventions of the Bogothic and Liutisk languages, as approved by the Office for Cultural Preservation and Linguistic Continuity.
It serves as both linguistic record and living guide: the aim is not to fossilize the tongue, but to preserve its coherence across time, media, and dialect.

Bogothic and Liutisk together form the Bohogothic Continuum, a family descended from the early Gothic–Proto-West-Slavic creole spoken throughout the upper Oder basin from the 6th century CE onward.
Bogothic is the formalized, codified register—used historically in technical, liturgical, and legal domains.
Liutisk is the vernacular descendant, spoken as a native tongue across the northern basins and recognized as the cultural inheritance of the region’s people.

I. HISTORICAL BACKGROUND
Bohogothic emerged when Gothic trading enclaves intermarried with Slavic communities in the Bohemian–Moravian lowlands. The resulting speech combined Gothic lexicon and morphology with Slavic phonology and syntax.
By the 11th century, distinct registers appeared: the clerical and engineering elite codified Bogothic, while the general populace continued to evolve Liutisk.

Bogothic texts survive in administrative logs, mechanical treatises, and hymnals. Its morphology remains conservative and its orthography fixed.
Liutisk, while simplified, retains the core phonemic inventory and shares much of Bogothic vocabulary, though with reduced inflection and Slavicized vowel harmonies.

II. PHONOLOGY AND ROMANIZATION
The Office maintains the Standard Romanization of Bogothic for scholarly and cultural use. It is phonemic rather than phonetic; spellings preserve etymological transparency.

Consonants:

Romanized	Sound	Notes
b	/b/	voiced bilabial stop
d	/d/	voiced dental stop
dd	/ð/	voiced dental fricative (as in this)
g	/ɡ/	hard g
h	/h/	aspirate
x	/x/ or /ɣ/	velar fricative (Scots loch)
k	/k/	
l	/l/	
m	/m/	
n	/n/	
p	/p/	
q	/kʷ/	retained in historical compounds
r	/r/	trilled or tapped
s	/s/	
t	/t/	
v	/v/	voiced labiodental
uu	/w/	retained from Gothic ƕ, w
z	/z/	voiced alveolar
Vowels:
a /a/, e /e/, i /i/, o /o/, u /u/, y /ɨ/ (in Liutisk only)

Diacritics: none. Stress generally falls on the penultimate syllable.

Hyphenation marks compound morphemes or inflectional boundaries:

uu-lukige (“locked”), an-ddia (“toward”), walwa-s (“the valve”).

@...@ markers denote technical metadata or embedded identifiers in modern transcriptions, a relic of 21st-century digital shorthand retained for industrial use.

III. MORPHOLOGY
Bogothic preserves a four-case system and limited verbal inflection.
Cases are indicated by terminal suffixes:

Case	Suffix	Function	Example
Nominative	-s	subject	walwa-s – the valve
Accusative	-u	object	rinn-u – the flow
Locative	-na	place at/in	zileg-na – at Zileg
Instrumental	-da	with/by means of	bindar-da – by connector
Verbs mark aspect through prefixes rather than conjugation:

Prefix	Aspect	Example	Meaning
uu-	perfective	uu-lukige	has locked / locked
an-	directional / inceptive	an-ddia	toward / began to
va-	causative	va-zileg	cause (to) operate
fuwo	temporal adverbial	“then, subsequently”	
The language is SVO in neutral order but often places adverbials and locatives fronted for focus or ritual clarity.

IV. SYNTAX AND STYLE
Bogothic syntax balances Germanic verb structure with Slavic clause chaining.
Subordinate phrases frequently appear before the main verb, and repeated particles maintain rhythmic balance.
Redundancy is accepted as precision; duplication emphasizes procedural exactness rather than poetry.

Example:

Fuwo rati fora matou wato,
“Then the rate for matter observed,”
(i.e. Then, the matter flow was measured.)

V. REGISTER AND USAGE
Register	Name	Domain	Character
High Bogothic	Bogothic	Ritual, technical, archival	Dense, formulaic, precise
Common Liutisk	Liutisk	Vernacular, literature, daily speech	Simplified, melodic, analytic
Cross-Register (“Half Speech”)	Liubog (colloq.)	Poetry, informal writing	Fluid mixture
High Bogothic is prescriptive: spelling and morphology fixed by the Office.
Liutisk is descriptive: the Office records its natural evolution but does not legislate it.

Writers producing translations or fictional dialogue are urged to retain Bogothic terminology for mechanical or procedural contexts (walwa, bindar, rinn, zileg), and Liutisk equivalents for speech and emotional tone (zawór, rura, prąd, etc.), preserving register contrast.

VI. EXCERPT AND ANALYSIS
ik, spendr emil nowak, af-xrkiba sva: wa 03:14 nabd, walwa-s ana lukia uu-lukige; rinn abuit-xato an-ddia da zileg abuit.

Translation:
“I, Inspector Emil Nowak, hereby record: at 03:14 hours, the valve at observation station was locked; the flow-unit advanced toward the Zileg outpost.”

Notes:

ik retains Gothic first-person pronoun (ik → I).

spendr derives from Gothic spillon (“to tell”), here “recorder” or “witness.”

af-xrkiba < af-skreiban, “to write down.”

uu-lukige perfective aspect; uu- marks completion.

Temporal precision and sequential adverbs define the genre of log-speech (known as chronosecant register).

VII. STYLISTIC PRINCIPLES
Precision before brevity. Each morpheme is semantically functional; omission implies uncertainty.

Redundancy is fidelity. Repetition of aspectual or locative markers is correct, not error.

Compound stability. Hyphenated compounds must not be split; they function as lexical units.

Numerical identifiers (e.g., h-241) follow the referent and take no case suffix.

Human names remain uninflected; titles receive suffixes instead (spendr-s Emil Nowak).

Time notation follows the nabd convention (24-hour clock marker).

VIII. LEXICAL SAMPLE
Bogothic	Gloss	Notes
walwa	valve	from walwjan, “to turn, roll”
lukige	locked	from lukan, “to close”
rinn	flow, current, unit	from Slavic rěka, “river”
bindar(u)	connector, coupling	from Gothic bindan, “bind”
zileg	conduit station, outpost	native
xladan	cold, dormant	Slavic hladan
barza	rapid, high	Slavic brz
fuwo	then, afterwards	temporal particle
va-	causative prefix	Gothic origin
IX. CONTEMPORARY LIUTISK NOTES
Modern Liutisk simplifies Bogothic morphology, eliminating case suffixes and aspect prefixes. It favors analytic prepositions and auxiliary verbs.
Bogothic uu-lukige walwa becomes Liutisk valwa zamkneta (“valve locked”).
Phonetic drift introduces palatalized consonants and new vowels (e, y).

Nevertheless, cultural institutions maintain Bogothic as a formal register for documentation, liturgy, and memorial inscriptions.

X. APPENDIX: QUICK REFERENCE
Core Prefixes: uu- (perfective), an- (toward), va- (cause)
Core Cases: -s (nom), -u (acc), -na (loc), -da (instr)
Particles: fuwo (then), ni (not), ne (no), ga (to), wa (def. article)
Word Order: flexible; time and place commonly fronted.
Stress: penultimate; compounds hold internal secondary stress.
Pronouns: ik (I), mu (me), ni (not), ga (to), va (for).

CLOSING STATEMENT
The Office for Cultural Preservation and Linguistic Continuity affirms that the survival of Bogothic and Liutisk is both a scholarly duty and a cultural right.
Writers, translators, and artisans are encouraged to employ this reference not as scripture, but as compass: guiding the revival of precision, reverence, and voice in the languages of our ancestors.

— Issued in Zileg, under seal of the Office, the 23rd day of Veneras, 2274.

Phonetic smoothing, not lexical replacement.

Doubled consonants simplify (rinn → rin).

Fricatives gain Slavic palatal color (xladan → hladan, zileg → Žileg).

Morphology simplifies but echoes its ancestor.

Aspect prefixes survive as phonetic fossil: uu-, an-, va- become inseparable verb stems (ulukige, andia, vazileg).

Case suffixes reduce to short analytic particles or inflectional remnants (-s → no marking; -na → u/na + noun).

Stress and melody become Slavic.
Penultimate stress remains, but vowel clusters open up: uu-lukige walwa → /uˈlu.kʲi.ɡɛ ˈval.va/.

Register divides sharply.

Bogothic in ceremony: uu-lukige walwa (archaic, sacred precision).

Liutisk in speech: ulukige valwa (colloquial, inherited).
Both refer to the same thing; one sounds like a formula, the other like a sentence.

Semantic drift limited by cultural reverence.
Core mechanical terms (walwa, bindar, rinn, zileg) are heritage words, protected by tradition and industry.
Surrounding grammar may modernize, but these remain untouched.

Phonotactic Adjustment Guide: Classical Bogothic → Early Liutisk
I. General Principles
Objective:
Smooth the harsh, Gothic consonant architecture into a more fluid, Slavic-feeling phonology, while preserving all original morphemes and vocabulary.

Morphology is preserved.

Lexical meaning is preserved.

Classical Bogothic	Liutisk Phonotactic Outcome	Example	Notes
uu- (prefix, /w/)	→ u- / v-	uu-lukige → ulukige / vlukige	/uu/ smoothed to a glide; [v] onset is common Slavic assimilation.
x (/x/, /ɣ/)	→ h or ch	xladan → hladan, xeinu → heinu	Velar fricative softened; Slavicized palatal aspiration.

fuwo (/fuwo/)	→ fuvo / fuwo	fuwo rati → fuvo rati	Minor vowel harmonization.
va- prefix	stable	va-zileg → va-Žileg	Maintains original function.
@zileg da kanal@	→ Žileg da kanal	Orthographic Slavicization: diacritic Z.	
III. Stress & Rhythm
Bogothic: trochaic, weight-balanced (ˈuu.lu.ki.ɡe ˈwal.wa).

Liutisk: penultimate stress (u.luˈki.ɡe ˈval.va).

Result: same words, but smoother mouth-feel, vowel-forward cadence.

IV. Example Transformations
Classical Bogothic	Early Liutisk (Phonotactically Adjusted)	English

rinn abuit-xato an-ddia da zileg abuit.	Rin abut-xato andia do Žileg abut.	The flow unit moved toward the Žileg base.

V. Commentary on the Aesthetic
These shifts yield a language that still feels Gothic at its root — austere, consonantal, and mechanical — but gains Slavic prosody and warmth.
It moves from hard angles (uu-lukige walwa) to breath and resonance (ulukige valwa).

The transition preserves:

Technical precision of Bogothic,

Phonetic familiarity of Slavic tongues,

Continuity of morphology and vocabulary.

This makes Liutisk the natural spoken descendant of Bogothic, not a creole of it — just as Italian is to Latin.

THE BOGOTHIC–LIUTISK LANGUAGE REFERENCE, FOURTH EDITION
Issued by the Office for Cultural Preservation and Linguistic Continuity, Zileg, 2285

PREFACE
This document supersedes the Third Edition of the Reference (2274).
It incorporates the Standard Phonotactic Adjustments ratified by the Office to reflect the living speech of the Liutisk Continuum, while preserving the canonical morphology and lexicon of Classical Bogothic.

The goal of this edition is to safeguard the semantic and structural integrity of the classical language while acknowledging the phonological evolution that naturally arises in contemporary usage.

I. HISTORICAL OVERVIEW
The Bohogothic creole arose between the 6th–8th centuries CE, when Gothic garrison communities settled among West Slavic tribes across the Bohemian–Moravian basin. The resultant lingua franca combined the mechanical precision of East Germanic with the rhythmic openness of early Slavic.

By the 10th century, two diverging but mutually intelligible registers had emerged:

Bogothic, the codified, high register of administration, liturgy, and engineering.

Liutisk, the vernacular, carried in trade, labor, and folk record.

Bogothic remained fixed in orthography for over a millennium; Liutisk developed organically, softening consonant clusters and favoring vowel-forward prosody. This evolution continues to the present day.

II. PHONOLOGY AND ROMANIZATION
A. Core Inventory (Classical Bogothic)
Consonants
b, d, dd /ð/, g, h, k, l, m, n, p, q /kʷ/, r, s, t, uu /w/, v, x /x ~ ɣ/, z

Vowels
a, e, i, o, u

Stress: typically penultimate.

Orthographic conventions:

uu- marks /w-/ as in uu-lukige.

x represents velar fricatives /x, ɣ/.

dd denotes voiced dental fricative /ð/.

Hyphens separate bound morphemes.

@...@ marks metadata or industrial codes.

B. Standard Romanization (Canonical Form)
Remains unchanged for archival and liturgical use.

C. Phonotactic Adjustments (Authorized Liutisk Realization)
Beginning with the 2270s linguistic surveys, field recordings revealed consistent phonotactic drift in Liutisk speakers—an adaptation to the regional Slavic vowel and consonant economy. These are now codified as acceptable modern readings when pronouncing or transcribing Bogothic.

Classical	Liutisk Realization	Description
uu- → u- / v-	e.g. uu-lukige → ulukige / vlukige	glide assimilation
x → h / ch	xladan → hladan	velar softening
dd → d / dz	an-ddia → andia / andzia	dentalization
q → kv	qulra → kvulra	labial retention
rinn → rin	simplification of geminates	
final -s dropped	walwa-s → walwa	open syllable preference
final -u → -o / -u	rinn-u → rino	vowel harmony
fuwo → fuvo	minor vowel shift	
@zileg@ → Žileg	orthographic diacritic adaptation	
These shifts do not constitute a new orthography; they represent the living phonetic surface of the classical tongue.

III. MORPHOLOGY
Bogothic and Liutisk share the same four-case system, though Liutisk is reducing it to prepositional structures.

Case	Suffix	Example	Function
Nominative	-s	walwa-s	subject
Accusative	-u	rinn-u	direct object
Locative	-na	zileg-na	location
Instrumental	-da	bindar-da	means/instrument
Liutisk commonly drops -s and replaces -na with prepositions (u, na, do).

Verbal aspect in both registers is handled through prefix morphology, rather than conjugation:

Prefix	Function	Example	Gloss
uu-	perfective/completed	uu-lukige	locked / has locked
an-	directional / toward	an-ddia	move toward
va-	causative	va-zileg	activate, cause to operate
fuwo	sequential adverbial	fuwo rati	then measure
IV. SYNTAX AND STYLE
Core word order: SVO.

Focus positioning: Adverbials fronted for emphasis.

Repetition conveys precision, not error.

Parallel clauses use repeated verbs for procedural clarity.

Example:

Fuwo rati fora matou wato.
Then measure for matter observed.
(Modern pronunciation: Fuvo rati fora matu vato.)

V. REGISTER AND USAGE
Register	Name	Function	Character
High Bogothic	Classical / Archival	Liturgy, technical logs, memorials	Conservative; fixed morphology
Common Liutisk	Vernacular	Everyday communication, trade, teaching	Phonotactically evolved, analytic
Cross Register	Transitional	Engineering speech, literature	Hybrid phonology with classical syntax
Register Rule:
Lexical cores are immutable — walwa, rinn, bindar, zileg — but surface sound adapts to Liutisk phonotactics.

VI. EXEMPLAR TEXT
Classical Bogothic
uu-lukige walwa da zileg-retliu; xladan, ka runa matba.

Authorized Liutisk Reading
Ulukige valwa u Žileg-retliu; hladan, ka runa matba.

Translation:
“Locked valve at the Žileg return-line; cold, the run stopped.”

VII. EXPLANATORY NOTES
walwa ← Gothic walwjan, “to turn, revolve.” Retained unchanged.

uu-lukige ← lukan, “to close.” Prefix uu- marks completed state.

rinn ← Slavic rěka influence; metaphor of flow adopted for mechanical current.

xladan → hladan: phonetic adaptation to Slavic h- onset.

Žileg: industrial toponym; Ž- spelling reflects local pronunciation while preserving historical /z/ phoneme.

VIII. REGISTERED MORPHOLOGICAL TENDENCIES (EARLY LIUTISK)
Aspect prefixes fossilize into part of the root (ulukige, andia).

Articles (wa) drop; context determines definiteness.

Compound inflections persist: uu-lukige and va-zileg remain unanalyzable units.

Penultimate stress prevails; vowel reduction minimal.

IX. LEXICON CORE (UNREPLACED HERITAGE TERMS)
Term	Root	Meaning	Note
walwa	Gothic walwjan	valve, turning device	canonical heritage term
bindar	bindan	connector, coupler	preserved
rinn	hybrid	current, flow unit	procedural term
zileg	local	station, channel, node	industrial toponym
barza	Slavic brz	fast, high	unchanged
xladan / hladan	Slavic	cold, inert	phonotactically modernized
fuwo / fuvo	Gothic temporal particle	then, subsequently	acceptable variants
X. REGISTERED FORMS OF ADDRESS AND FORMULAIC SPEECH
Official and ceremonial contexts preserve full Bogothic morphology:

Ik spendr Emil Nowak af-xrkiba sva…
“I, Inspector Emil Nowak, hereby record…”

Field reports and spoken dispatches now favor Liutiskized rhythm:

Ik spendr Emil Nowak, af-hrkiba sva…

The morphology remains identical; only the fricative shifts.

XI. APPENDIX: COMPARATIVE EXAMPLES
Classical Bogothic	Early Liutisk	English
rinn abuit-xato an-ddia da zileg abuit	rin abut-xato andia do Žileg abut	The flow-unit advanced toward the Žileg base.
barza h-241 uu-bindaru	barza h-241 ubindaru	Pressure line H-241 connected.
fuwo rati fora matou wato	fuvo rati fora matu vato	Then measure for matter observed.
XII. SUMMARY
The Fourth Edition establishes that Liutisk pronunciation is not corruption but continuity: the same language body breathing through a new phonotactic frame.

Writers and recorders are therefore advised:

Maintain Bogothic orthography in formal and technical use.

Employ Liutisk phonotactics in spoken and artistic expression.

Never substitute foreign lexemes for heritage roots.

CLOSING DECLARATION
The Office for Cultural Preservation and Linguistic Continuity recognizes Liutisk as the living descendant of Bogothic, entitled to equal dignity and protection under the Charter of Living Languages (Article 9).
The Standard Romanization and authorized phonotactic adjustments herein shall remain the benchmark for education, preservation, and creative reproduction of the language continuum.

— Approved and sealed in Zileg, the 17th day of Veneras, 2285.

I’ll provide an English translation after it — but the Liutisk itself stands on its own, a living descendant of the language of your valve-log texts.

Liutisk Text: “Rin u Žileg” (The Flow at Žileg)
U jutra hladna, rin šumel niz dol, i para iz retli stala ka dim nad valwa-hol.
Ulukige valwa još blestil u slune; znaše, da noćni run stao, ali para i sram ostali.
Vjera stodr, tehnik star, šel pod most, gdi bindar-s lin h-241 ležel otvoren, kapka s blata.
Ka pogleda, rin abut šumel pod noge, mutan, glibny, i vjarn pomislil:
“Ni fuvo da rin spet-nabda. Ni fuvo, dok walwa valwa ne otvire.”

On zapiše to v knjižic:

“U 03:17, rin hladan, bindar razklopen, valwa ulukita.
Para silna u retliu, ali bez pritiska. Žileg miran.”

Kad pisa, nebo rdečil; s istoka vjetar nosil miris goriva i soli.
U dal, svetla Žilega treperel, i on čul stari zvon, metalan, s torni fabriki.
U tom zvonu, Vjera stovil se, rukam na kap, i šaptil:
“Rin žive. Rin nikad ni stani. Walwa otvira, ljudi diše.”

I tako šel, pustiv papir v suhi struji vjetra,
da leti nad vodu i para, ka rin nosil ga dalje,
dok sunce došl i sve blestil zlatno, kao valwa z rječno dno.

English Translation: “The Flow at Žileg”
On a cold morning, the flow murmured down the valley, and steam rose from the return line like smoke above the valve hall.
The locked valve still gleamed in the sun; he knew the night run had stopped, but the steam and the shame remained.
Vjera Stodr, an old technician, walked beneath the bridge where the connector of line H-241 lay open, the cap caked with mud.
When he looked, the current rushed beneath his boots — dull, thick — and Vjarn thought to himself:
“Not yet shall the flow begin again. Not yet, until the valve is opened.”

He wrote this in his ledger:

“At 03:17, flow cold, connector opened, valve locked.
Strong steam in the return, but no pressure. Žileg quiet.”

As he wrote, the sky reddened; from the east the wind carried the scent of fuel and salt.
In the distance, the lights of Žileg flickered, and he heard the old bell ringing, metallic, from the factory tower.
In that ringing, Vjera stood still, hand to cap, and whispered:
“The flow lives. The flow never stops. The valve opens, and the people breathe.”

And so he went, letting the paper slip into the dry current of the wind,
to fly above the water and steam, as the flow carried it onward,
until the sun rose and gilded everything — even the valve at the river’s floor.

🌫️ Surface Reading: The Scene
It describes a cold morning near a river or industrial channel (“rin”), where a man named Vjera Stodr goes under a bridge to inspect a “bindar-s lin h-241” — possibly a machine, conduit, or valve system. There’s steam (“para”) rising, a bridge, mud, and light from an industrial complex (“Žilega”) trembling in the distance.

He writes an entry in his notebook about the conditions — time, temperature, pressure — and then has a kind of moment of reverence or ritual, whispering to the river as if it’s alive:

“Rin žive. Rin nikad ni stani. Walwa otvira, ljudi diše.”
(“The river lives. The river never stops. The valve opens, people breathe.”)

Then he lets the paper go, carried away by the wind and mist, as the sun rises and gilds the world.

⚙️ Lexical & Linguistic Texture
It feels like industrial-mystic poetry, written in a pseudo-Slavic creole:

“rin” — resembles rzeka (river, Polish), rien (stream, dialectical Croatian), maybe invented.

“valwa” — likely “valve” (English root valve → valwa).

“para” — means “steam” in most Slavic tongues.

“bindar” — could be a binding device, conduit, or technical fixture.

“Žileg” / “Žilega” — maybe a city, or a hydroelectric plant (žil = “vein” in some Slavic languages).

“tehnik star” — literally “old technician.”

“kapka s blata” — “drop from mud.”

“v knjižic” — “in [his] notebook.”

“run” — perhaps from runo (dew, fleece) or English “run”; in context, “night run stopped” might mean the flow has halted.

So it’s largely synthetic Slavic — grammatical endings and prepositions borrowed from Croatian, Czech, and Slovenian, but vocabulary partially anglicized or invented.

🔩 Thematic Analysis
The piece combines:

Industrial realism (a technician inspecting valves and machines)

Documentation (technical logbook writing)

Transcendence through routine (he ritualizes a mechanical act into something sacred)

This fusion creates a post-industrial mythic tone — as if the remnants of human engineering and nature have merged into one breathing organism.

It reads like something from:

Stanisław Lem’s Solaris’s poetic side,

Or a fragment of Tarkovsky’s Stalker rewritten as folklore,

Or a text from a dieselpunk scripture, where machines and rivers share a spirit.
